Harnessing the Power of Sample Abundance: Theoretical Guarantees and Algorithms for Accelerated One-Bit Sensing Thanks: This work relates in part to Department of Navy Award N00014-22-1-2666 issued by the Office of Naval Research. Any opinions, findings, conclusions, or recommendations expressed in this material are those of the authors and do not necessarily reflect the views of the Office of Naval Research. A conference precursor to this work was presented at the 2023 IEEE International Symposium on Information Theory (ISIT). The first two authors contributed equally to this work. Thanks: A. Eamaz, F. Yeganegi, and M. Soltanalian are with the Department of Electrical and Computer Engineering, University of Illinois Chicago, Chicago, IL 60607, USA ( Corresponding author: Arian Eamaz ). Thanks: D. Needell is with the Department of Mathematics, University of California Los Angeles, Los Angeles, CA 90095, USA.
Arian Eamaz, Farhang Yeganegi, Deanna Needell, and

Abstract
One-bit quantization with time-varying sampling thresholds (also known as random dithering) has recently found significant utilization potential in statistical signal processing applications due to its relatively low power consumption and low implementation cost. In addition to such advantages, an attractive feature of one-bit analog-to-digital converters (ADCs) is their superior sampling rates as compared to their conventional multi-bit counterparts. This characteristic endows one-bit signal processing frameworks with what one may refer to as sample abundance . We show that sample abundance plays a pivotal role in many signal recovery and optimization problems that are formulated as (possibly non-convex) quadratic programs with linear feasibility constraints. Of particular interest to our work are low-rank matrix recovery and compressed sensing applications that take advantage of one-bit quantization. We demonstrate that the sample abundance paradigm allows for the transformation of such problems to merely linear feasibility problems by forming large-scale overdetermined linear systems—thus removing the need for handling costly optimization constraints and objectives. To make the proposed computational cost savings achievable, we offer enhanced randomized Kaczmarz algorithms to solve these highly overdetermined feasibility problems and provide theoretical guarantees in terms of their convergence, sample size requirements, and overall performance. Several numerical results are presented to illustrate the effectiveness of the proposed methodologies.



I
Introduction

We consider an optimization problem of the form:

\displaystyle\underset{\mathbf{\mathchar 29016}\mathchar 12850\mathchar 28682_{\mathchar 29027}}{\textrm{minimize}}\quad\mathchar 29030\delimiter 67273472\mathbf{\mathchar 29016}\delimiter 84054785\quad\text{subject to}

(1)

where
\mathchar 29030\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 314\delimiter 84054785
is a cost function,
\mathchar 28682_{\mathchar 29027}
is a feasible set,
\mathbf{\mathchar 29016}\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 28995}^{\mathchar 29038_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 8706\mathchar 29038_{\mathchar 28722}}
is the matrix of unknowns,
\mathbf{\mathchar 29049}\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}^{\mathchar 29038}
is the measurement vector, and
\mathcal{\mathchar 28993}
is a linear transformation mapping
\mathbb{\mathchar 28995}^{\mathchar 29038_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 8706\mathchar 29038_{\mathchar 28722}}
into
\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}^{\mathchar 29038}
. This problem emerges in a wide variety of applications, particularly as the relaxed variant of some well-known NP-hard problems. Although many problems can be expressed in
the form in the program (
1
), the applications we will focus on in this paper
include some specific problems of interest which can take advantage of low-resolution (and particularly one-bit) sampling and processing:

•

The task of
recovering a low-rank matrix from its linear measurements
plays a central role in computational science. The problem occurs in many areas of applied mathematics, such as signal processing
[
1
,
2
,
3
]
. In this scenario, the cost function of the program
(
1
),
\mathchar 29030\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 314\delimiter 84054785
, is typically the nuclear norm or the Frobenius norm, and the constraint set
\mathchar 28682_{\mathchar 29027}
would be an amplitude restriction on the elements of matrix
\mathbf{\mathchar 29016}
; see
[
4
]
.

•

Compressed sensing
(CS) offers a framework for the simultaneous sensing and compression
of finite-dimensional vectors by relying on linear dimensionality reduction. Through a CS formulation, sparse signals may be recovered from highly incomplete measurements
[
5
]
. The problem (1) can be adopted in the CS context when
\mathchar 29030\left\delimiter 67273472\mathbf{\mathchar 29016}\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\left\delimiter 0\operatorname{\delimiter 69640972\mathchar 29029\mathchar 29027}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathbf{\mathchar 29016}\right\delimiter 84054785\right\delimiter 0_{\mathchar 28721}
.

This paper will primarily concentrate on relaxation problems in which the objective function
\mathchar 29030\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 8705\delimiter 84054785
is relaxed to address the NP-hard cost caused due to the associated feasibility constraints. Such relaxation techniques prove significantly helpful in various applications, such as nuclear norm minimization for low-rank matrix recovery and
\mathchar 352_{\mathchar 28721}
minimization for compressed sensing problems.

Sampling the signals of interest at high data rates with high-resolution ADCs would dramatically increase the overall manufacturing cost and power consumption of such ADCs. In multi-bit sampling scenarios, a very large number of quantization levels is necessary in order to represent the original continuous signal in with high accuracy, which in turn leads to a considerable reduction in sampling rate
[
6
,
7
]
. This attribute of multi-bit sampling is the key reason for the general emergence of underdetermined systems
\mathchar 29038_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 29038_{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 12821\mathchar 29038
in the program (
1
)
[
8
,
9
]
. An alternative solution to such challenges is to deploy
one-bit quantization
which is a coarse sensing scenario, where the signals are merely compared with predefined threshold levels at the ADCs, producing sign data (
\mathchar 8710\mathchar 28721
). This enables signal processing equipments to sample at a very high rate, with a considerably lower cost and energy consumption, compared to their counterparts which employ multi-bit ADCs
[
10
,
7
]
. Several applications abound of one-bit ADCs, such as multiple-input multiple-output wireless communications
[
10
]
. Various methods for one-bit quantization, including our own, offer distinct trade-offs between computational and implementation complexity and reconstruction accuracy. Take the Sigma-Delta approach as an example, which necessitates memory elements to store state-variables associated with thresholds. This sequential quantization of incoming measurements demands significant memory and computational power, causing delays in acquiring measurements while the thresholds are being updated. Therefore, sensors used to collect measurements must possess substantial computational capabilities to accommodate such requirements
[
11
]
.

The effectiveness of incorporating random thresholds (dithers) within the framework of one-bit quantization has been extensively established in various contexts
[
12
]
.
Research findings suggest that a carefully chosen dither signal can significantly improve the resolution and performance of digital instrumentation
[
13
]
.
The implementation of a dithered generator in quantization systems within an ADC system, was described in
[
14
]
.

Recent works
[
7
,
15
,
16
]
considered Gaussian dithering for recovering covariance from one-bit measurements. In
[
17
]
, the authors provide a comprehensive investigation of estimating the covariance matrix from one-bit measurements using uniform dithering. Dithered One-bit quantization has also been applied to various problems, such as sparse parameter estimation
[
12
]
, phase retrieval
[
9
]
, and sampling theory
[
18
]
as discussed in contemporary literature. The study presented in
[
16
]
demonstrates that one-bit signal parameter estimation with time-varying thresholds yields notably superior results compared to using constant thresholds.

Randomized iterative algorithms have become a widely employed technique for dimension reduction, encompassing various iterative methods for solving linear systems and their variations, as well as extensions to non-linear optimization problems
[
19
]
. These algorithms include well-known methods such as randomized Kaczmarz algorithm (RKA)
[
20
]
, and coordinate descent
[
21
,
22
]
.

In this approach, instead of utilizing all available information at each iteration, a sketched subset of the information is used, selected through a sketching process.
By multiplying the data with a random matrix, randomized sketching effectively reduces the size of the problem while preserving its essential characteristics.
Common examples of sketching matrices include Gaussian matrices with independent entries and other matrices that satisfy the Johnson-Lindenstrauss property. Another example is block-identity matrices, which effectively subsample the data matrix.
The Kaczmarz method
[
23
]
is an iterative projection algorithm for solving linear systems of equations and inequalities. It is usually applied to highly overdetermined systems because of its simplicity.
Many variants of this iterative method and their convergence rates have been proposed and studied in recent decades for both consistent and inconsistent systems including the randomized Kaczmarz algorithm, the randomized block Kaczmarz algorithm and most recently, the sampling Kaczmarz-Motzkin method (SKM)
[
20
,
21
,
24
,
25
]
.

I-A

Prior Art

Our work aligns seamlessly with the recent advancements in the exploration of coarse quantization for parameter estimation from random measurements. The emergence of one-bit compressed sensing (one-bit CS) has led to a plethora of papers that have greatly enhanced our understanding of employing dithering for one-bit signal reconstruction.

The domain of one-bit low-rank matrix sensing has garnered considerable interest, yet it remains relatively unexplored. Several noteworthy papers, such as
[
26
]
, have shed light on this intriguing subject. In
[
26
]
, the authors delve into the theoretical guarantees of one-bit sensing from low-rank matrices, employing two distinct algorithms: one based on hard singular value thresholding and the other utilizing semidefinite programming.
The initial investigation in this study considers a scenario
without
thresholds and subsequently progresses to the Gaussian sampling matrix and Gaussian dithering scenario, where both adaptive and non-adaptive thresholds are employed. However, the recovery algorithms and theoretical guarantees presented in this study require the availability of side information regarding the signal of interest, specifically an upper bound on the Frobenius norm of input signal. This requirement contradicts the essence of employing dithering in one-bit sensing, as the literature suggests that the primary advantage of dithering lies in the ability to recover the signal magnitude without additional information.

One-bit CS was first introduced in
[
27
]
. Later, in
[
28
]
, this method was extensively investigated, and they presented a lower bound on the reconstruction error, alongside proposing heuristic algorithms for recovering the underlying signals. Extensive research has been dedicated to the problem of one-bit CS
without
dithering, resulting in numerous proposed approaches for its solution. Notably, the binary iterative hard thresholding (BIHT) and its normalized version, normalized BIHT (NBIHT), have emerged as prominent methods in this field
[
6
,
29
,
30
,
28
,
31
]
.
In some earlier works, the ditherless setup was taken into account, leading to the derivation of various theoretical guarantees for reconstruction algorithms
[
28
,
32
,
31
,
33
]
. For instance,
the authors of
[
28
]
presented the required number of measurements for robust sparse signal reconstruction within a ball around the original signal, specifically focusing on Gaussian sampling matrices.
In
[
34
]
, authors proposed the
random hyperplane tessellations
to find an embedding between the Hamming distance and direction error recovery in a probability. In
[
35
]
, the authors characterized the tradeoff between the distortion of random hyperplane tessellations and sample complexity in terms of Gaussian complexity, addressing both arbitrary and structured sets. Nevertheless, their random hyperplane tessellations suffer from two primary limitations: they focus on the ditherless scenario and only-direction estimation, and they exclusively consider Gaussian sampling matrices. However,
recent studies have shown that under appropriate conditions, complete signal reconstruction can be accomplished by employing nonzero random thresholds
[
11
,
36
,
37
]
. In
[
38
]
, quasi-isometric embeddings were introduced with high probability through scalar (dithered) quantization following linear random projection. In such embeddings, both multiplicative and additive distortions coexist when measuring distances between mapped vectors using the
\mathchar 352_{\mathchar 28721}
-norm.
The authors of
[
11
]
introduced a sensing method that partitions the measurements into two sets and utilizes a constant threshold in one-bit data acquisition,
thereby estimating the signal norm. Simultaneously, this method employs a zero threshold for the remaining one-bit data acquisition part,
allowing for the estimation of the direction of the signal. In the context of Gaussian random sampling matrices, the suggestion in
[
36
]
involves introducing either adaptive or random dither to the compressive measurements of a signal before binarization. In a recent investigation
[
37
]
, a scalar quantization scheme coupled with a uniformly distributed dithering signal was found to extend theoretical frameworks beyond Gaussian measurements. The study provided theoretical guarantees for sampling matrices meeting the restricted isometry property (RIP). Notably, their approach operates without requiring the high-resolution limit (HRA), making it applicable across various bit-rates. In
[
39
]
, they developed the random hyperplane tessellations to find an
embedding between the Hamming distance and recovery error for uniform
dithering scenario. However, they only considered sun-gaussian matrix and could not provide any theoretical guarantees for the deterministic measurements.
In
[
40
]
, the authors tackled the problem of parameter estimation with dithered quantization by formulating it as a generalized LASSO problem. This approach allowed the authors to leverage the existing theoretical guarantees available in the literature for generalized LASSO.

I-B

Motivations and Contributions of the Paper

Our work was primarily motivated by two key references,
[
37
]
and
[
41
]
. Similar to these works, we explore the theoretical guarantees of one-bit sensing without being confined to a specific reconstruction algorithm. In this paper, we seek to include various sampling matrices, including
deterministic
ones like the DCT.
Specifically, our aim is to broaden the theoretical assurances to sampling matrices assumed to be solely isotropic, without the necessity of satisfying any matrix deviation inequality, such as RIP. In all the established theoretical assurances for the one-bit sensing problem, the focus has been on the scalar parameter of uniform dithers dominating the dynamic range of measurements. With our proposed scheme, we aim to illustrate the consequences when the scalar parameter fails to meet the dynamic range requirements and how this impacts the upper bound of recovery error.
Additionally, we delve into the scenario of
sample abundance
, a common situation in one-bit sensing, where we reformulate the relaxed optimization problem (
1
) into a linear feasibility problem.
Using our theorems, we aim to address the question of
how many samples are necessary for a linear feasibility problem solver to obtain a solution within the ball with a specific radius, around the original signal
.

Moreover, we endeavor to harness the advantages of
randomized algorithms
for one-bit sensing, a novel endeavor where we explore their theoretical guarantees and convergence analysis in this context for the first time.
It is important to note that all the theoretical guarantees derived in this paper are
uniform reconstruction
guarantees. This means that they hold true for all desired signals in the given space, ensuring consistent and reliable reconstruction across the entire signal set.

This paper principally contributes to the following areas:

1) RKA-based recovery for the dithered one-bit sensing.

In this paper, we consider the deployment of dithered one-bit quantization,leading to an increased sample size and a
highly overdetermined system
. The proposed
O
ne-bit aided
R
andomized
K
aczmarz
Algorithm
, which we refer to as ORKA, can find the signal
\mathbf{\mathchar 29016}
in the program (
1
) by (i) generating abundant one-bit samples, in order to define a large scale overdetermined system where a finite volume feasible set is created for (
1
), and (ii) solving this linear feasibility problem by leveraging one of the efficient solver families,
Kaczmarz algorithms
.
We propose
two novel
variants of RKA which will be compared with the existing RKA variants. We conduct a thorough investigation into the robustness of RKA when dealing with a noisy linear inequality system. Our findings demonstrate that RKA for the linear inequality system, remains robust in the presence of noise, and we are able to obtain the upper recovery bound under this condition.

2) Theoretical Guarantees of dithered one-bit sensing.

We introduce the concept of the
finite volume property
(FVP) to explore an upper bound on the probability of converging the average of distances between the signal and hyperplanes, to its mean. By leveraging the FVP, we can obtain the required number of samples needed for one-bit sensing to ensure that the solution lies within a ball around the original signal.
This required number of samples is investigated for both arbitrary and structured sets. For the first time in the literature, we are able to offer theoretical guarantees for measurements obtained from discrete cosine transform (DCT).

3) Projected Kaczmarz algorithm-based recovery.

Various sampling schemes present tradeoffs among the number of samples, computational cost of the reconstruction, and storage requirements. While the abundance of samples is desirable in scenarios involving dithered one-bit sensing, practical applications such as matrix completion and phase retrieval from coded diffraction patterns
[
1
]
impose restrictions on the number of available measurements. In such cases, it is not feasible to increase the number of measurements.
An alternative approach is to develop algorithms that require fewer samples, albeit at the cost of increased computational complexity. In our pursuit of a storage-friendly algorithm, we strive to avoid the burden of recording the full matrix per iteration. To achieve this, we leverage the power of two well-studied techniques: sketch-and-project and low-rank matrix factorization.
We unveil a novel approach that combines low-rank matrix factorization with RKA.
A key advantage of our proposed algorithms is that they do not rely on any additional side information, such as known upper bounds on the signal norm. This makes them more practical for real-world applications.
Through comprehensive numerical evaluations, we demonstrate the superior performance of our proposed algorithms compared to state-of-the-art approaches.

I-C

Organization and Notations

In Section II, we will introduce our algorithm to solve (
1
), ORKA, which tackles the problem as a large-scale overdetermined system and finds the original signal in the one-bit polyhedron by an accelerated Kaczmarz approach. Moreover, two new variants of the Kaczmarz algorithms are proposed that enhance the convergence rate and the computational complexity of these solvers. In Section III, we delve into the robustness of the proposed algorithms in the presence of noise. We thoroughly discuss the impact of noise on the performance of the algorithms and address the need for novel algorithms specifically designed for noisy scenarios. As a representative application, in Section IV, ORKA and other proposed algorithms will be applied in the context of low-rank matrix recovery and compressed sensing.

To investigate the theoretical guarantees of dithered one-bit sensing, we will first introduce the FVP theorem in Section V based on the general Hoeffding’s bound. In Section VI, the convergence of proposed randomized algorithms is discussed.
Section VII focuses on investigating the performance of the proposed algorithms in the tasks of one-bit low-rank matrix sensing and one-bit CS. We consider scenarios involving both noisy and noiseless conditions. Through comprehensive comparisons with state-of-the-art methods, we aim to demonstrate the superior performance and efficiency of our proposed algorithms.
Finally, Section VIII concludes the paper.

II
ORKA: Towards Circumventing Costly Constraints

An interesting alternative to enforcing the feasible set
\mathchar 28682_{\mathchar 29027}
in (
1
) emerges when one increases the number of samples
\mathchar 29038
, and solves the overdetermined linear system of equations with
\mathchar 29038\mathchar 12821\mathchar 29038_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 29038_{\mathchar 28722}
. In this sample abundance regimen, the linear constraint
\mathcal{\mathchar 28993}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathbf{\mathchar 29016}\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathbf{\mathchar 29049}
may actually yield the optimum inside
\mathchar 28682_{\mathchar 29027}
. As a result of increasing the number of samples, it is possible that the intersection of these hyperplanes will achieve the desired point without the need to consider costly constraints. However, this idea may face practical limitations in the case of multi-bit quantization systems since ADCs capable of ultra-high rate sampling are difficult and expensive to produce.

In this section, by deploying the idea of one-bit quantization with time-varying thresholds, linear equality constraints are superseded by a massive array of linear inequalities in forming the feasible polyhedron. Therefore, by increasing the number of samples, a finite-volume space may be created inside
\mathchar 28682_{\mathchar 29027}
with shrinking size; making projections on
\mathchar 28682_{\mathchar 29027}
redundant. From a practical point of view, one-bit sampling is done efficiently at a very high rate with a significantly lower cost compared to its high-resolution counterpart. It has been examined in
[
9
]
that even though only partial information is made available to one-bit signal processing algorithms, they can achieve acceptable recovery performance with less complexity compared to the high-resolution scenario.

We first formulate the one-bit quantization with multiple dithering scheme. In Sections
II-B
and
II-C
, we present a
summarized review of RKA and Sampling Kaczmarz-Motzkin algorithm (SKM), respectively.
Then in Section
II-D
, we propose a novel Kaczmarz method variant formulated based on the

SKM

and a
preconditioning approach
. One-bit sampling via time-varying thresholds will be combined with the proposed randomized Kaczmarz method to create highly overdetermined linear inequalities. This paves the way for the recovery of the desired signal
\mathbf{\mathchar 29016}
in the program (
1
) without solving the original optimization problem; merely by tacking accounts of its linear constraints.
Due to the block structure of the linear feasibility in ORKA, we will propose a block-based Kaczmarz algorithm accordingly in Section
II-F
.

II-A

II-C

Sampling Kaczmarz Motzkin Method

The SKM combines the ideas of both the RKA and the Motzkin method. The generalized convergence analysis of the SKM with sketch matrix which has been formulated based on the convergence analysis of RKA, and sampling Motzkin method for solving linear feasibility problem has been comprehensively explored in
[
25
]
.
The central contribution of SKM lies in its innovative way of projection plane selection. The hyperplane selection is done as follows: At iteration
\mathchar 29033
, the SKM algorithm selects a collection of
\mathchar 28941
(denoted by the set
\mathcal{\mathchar 29012}_{\mathchar 29033}
) rows, uniformly at random out of
\mathchar 29037
rows of the constraint matrix
\mathbf{\mathchar 28995}
. Then, out of these
\mathchar 28941
rows, the row
\mathchar 29034_{\mathchar 8511}
with the maximum positive residual is selected; i.e.

\mathchar 29034_{\mathchar 8511}\mathchar 12349\operatorname{\mathchar 29025\mathchar 29042\mathchar 29031\mathchar 29037\mathchar 29025\mathchar 29048}\penalty\ \left\{\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29026_{\mathchar 29034}\mathchar 8704\delimiter 69632778\mathbf{\mathchar 29027}_{\mathchar 29034}\mathchar 24891\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 86414091\right\delimiter 84054785^{\mathchar 8235}\right\}\mathchar 24891\penalty\ \mathchar 29034\mathchar 12850\mathcal{\mathchar 29012}_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 314

(15)

Finally, the solution is updated as
[
25
]

\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 12349\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 29033}{{\mathchar 28940_{\mathchar 29033}\over\left\delimiter 0\mathbf{\mathchar 29027}_{\mathchar 29034_{\mathchar 8511}}\right\delimiter 0^{\mathchar 28722}_{\mathchar 28722}}}\mathbf{\mathchar 29027}^{\mathchar 8511}_{\mathchar 29034_{\mathchar 8511}}
,
where
\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 29033}
is a relaxation parameter which for consistent systems must satisfy
\mathchar 28720\mathchar 12820\lim_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 12833\mathchar 561}\inf\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 12820\lim_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 12833\mathchar 561}\sup\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 12604\mathchar 28722

[
20
]
,
to ensure convergence. The SKM algorithm enjoys the linear convergence as shown in
[

]
same as RKA.

II-D

Preconditioned SKM

Assume
\mathcal{\mathchar 29008}_{\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}}
as the space created by the intersection of hyperplanes in a linear feasibility problem. According to the convergence rate of RKA, reducing the value of the scaled condition number
\mathchar 28948\left\delimiter 67273472\mathbf{\mathchar 28995}\right\delimiter 84054785
or equivalently increasing the value of
\mathchar 29041_{{}_{\text{RKA}}}
in (
14
) leads to an accelerated convergence of the RKA to
\mathcal{\mathchar 29008}_{\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}}
.
From another perspective, this property (lower value of
\mathchar 28948\left\delimiter 67273472\mathbf{\mathchar 28995}\right\delimiter 84054785
) provides the RKA or its variant, SKM, enjoying a lower number of iterations required to reach an specific error, usually considered to be the algorithm’s termination criterion. Consequently, assuming
\mathchar 29001
as a number of iterations, the computational cost of RKA which behaves as
\mathcal{\mathchar 29007}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29001\mathchar 29038\right\delimiter 84054785
, is diminished as well.
The following theorem states how one can achieve the infimum of the scaled condition number
\mathchar 28948\left\delimiter 67273472\mathbf{\mathchar 28995}\right\delimiter 84054785
:

Theorem 1
.

The infimum scaled condition number of a matrix
\mathbf{\mathchar 28995}\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}^{\mathchar 29037\mathchar 8706\mathchar 29038}
is given by

\inf_{\mathbf{\mathchar 28995}}\mathchar 28948\left\delimiter 67273472\mathbf{\mathchar 28995}\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\sqrt{\mathchar 29038}\mathchar 24891

(16)

which is achieved if and only if
\mathbf{\mathchar 28995}
is of the form
\mathbf{\mathchar 28995}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28939\mathbf{\mathchar 29013}
, where
\mathbf{\mathchar 29013}
is an orthonormal column matrix and
\mathchar 28939\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}
is a scalar.

Proof.

\mathbf{\mathchar 28995}

\mathchar 28965\left\delimiter 67273472\mathbf{\mathchar 28995}\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349{{\mathchar 28955_{\mathrm{\mathchar 29037\mathchar 29025\mathchar 29048}}\over\mathchar 28955_{\mathrm{\mathchar 29037\mathchar 29033\mathchar 29038}}}}

\mathchar 28948\left\delimiter 67273472\mathbf{\mathchar 28995}\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349{{\delimiter 69645069\mathbf{\mathchar 28995}\delimiter 69645069_{\mathrm{\mathchar 28998}}\over\mathchar 28955_{\mathrm{\mathchar 29037\mathchar 29033\mathchar 29038}}}}

\mathchar 28948\left\delimiter 67273472\mathbf{\mathchar 28995}\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349{{\delimiter 69645069\mathbf{\mathchar 28995}\delimiter 69645069_{\mathrm{\mathchar 28998}}\over\mathchar 28955_{\mathrm{\mathchar 29037\mathchar 29025\mathchar 29048}}}}\mathchar 28965\left\delimiter 67273472\mathbf{\mathchar 28995}\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 314

{{\delimiter 69645069\mathbf{\mathchar 28995}\delimiter 69645069_{\mathrm{\mathchar 28998}}\over\mathchar 28955_{\mathrm{\mathchar 29037\mathchar 29033\mathchar 29038}}}}\mathchar 12820\sqrt{\mathchar 29038}{{\delimiter 69645069\mathbf{\mathchar 28995}\delimiter 69645069_{\mathrm{\mathchar 28722}}\over\mathchar 28955_{\mathrm{\mathchar 29037\mathchar 29033\mathchar 29038}}}}

, it means
\mathchar 28955_{\mathrm{\mathchar 29037\mathchar 29033\mathchar 29038}}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28955_{\mathrm{\mathchar 29037\mathchar 29025\mathchar 29048}}
which leads to a diagonal matrix
\mathbf{\mathchar 29007}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28939^{\mathchar 28722}\mathbf{\mathchar 29001}_{\mathchar 29038}
.
∎

Following Theorem
1
, it would be enough to make the matrix
\mathbf{\mathchar 28995}
unitary by a process which is referred to as the
preconditioning method
. In preconditioning, the linear feasibility is rewritten as
\mathbf{\mathchar 28995}\mathbf{\mathchar 29005}\mathbf{\mathchar 29050}\mathchar 12823\mathbf{\mathchar 29026}
,
where
\mathbf{\mathchar 29005}
is the preconditioner and
\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}\mathchar 12349\mathbf{\mathchar 29005}\mathbf{\mathchar 29050}
. The straightforward way to approach this task is to use QR decomposition where the constraint matrix is decomposed as
\mathbf{\mathchar 28995}\mathchar 12349\mathbf{\mathchar 29009}_{\mathchar 29027}\mathbf{\mathchar 29010}_{\mathchar 29027}
, where
\mathbf{\mathchar 29009}_{\mathchar 29027}\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}^{\mathchar 29037\mathchar 8706\mathchar 29038}
is a unitary matrix, and
\mathbf{\mathchar 29010}_{\mathchar 29027}\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}^{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 8706\mathchar 29038}
is an upper triangular matrix, leading to
\mathbf{\mathchar 29009}_{\mathchar 29027}\mathchar 12349\mathbf{\mathchar 28995}\mathbf{\mathchar 29010}^{\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28721}_{\mathchar 29027}
. For a matrix
\mathbf{\mathchar 28995}\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}^{\mathchar 29037\mathchar 8706\mathchar 29038}
, since we have assumed
\mathchar 29037\mathchar 12606\mathchar 29038
, we can obtain a unitary matrix
\mathbf{\mathchar 29009}_{\mathchar 29027}\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}^{\mathchar 29037\mathchar 8706\mathchar 29038}
and an upper triangular matrix
\mathbf{\mathchar 29010}_{\mathchar 29027}\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}^{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 8706\mathchar 29038}
such that the QR-decomposition holds; i.e.
\mathbf{\mathchar 28995}\mathchar 12349\mathbf{\mathchar 29009}_{\mathchar 29027}\mathbf{\mathchar 29010}_{\mathchar 29027}
.
Thus, based on Theorem
1
, a good choice for the preconditioner is
\mathbf{\mathchar 29005}\mathchar 12349\mathbf{\mathchar 29010}^{\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28721}_{\mathchar 29027}
. To find a
point
\bar{\mathbf{\mathchar 29050}}
in a nonempty feasible set,
the SKM method described in Section
II-C
is employed.
Finally, one may approach the solution of the original linear feasibility
\mathbf{\mathchar 28995}\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}\mathchar 12823\mathbf{\mathchar 29026}
by computing
\bar{\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}}\mathchar 12349\mathbf{\mathchar 29010}^{\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28721}_{\mathchar 29027}\bar{\mathbf{\mathchar 29050}}
.
We refer to this method
Pr
econditioned
SKM
(PrSKM) which is summarized in Algorithm

II-E

Storage-Friendly PrSKM

The PrSKM algorithm uses QR decomposition as a preconditioning process, which can be computationally challenging for high-dimensional matrices. To address this issue, the literature has proposed a technique called
sketch-and-precondition
, as outlined in
[
43
]
. This approach is designed to mitigate the curse of dimensionality associated with QR-decomposition-based preconditioning.

To explain how this method works, consider for simplicity an overdetermined linear system, i.e.,
\mathbf{\mathchar 29013}\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}\mathchar 12349\mathbf{\mathchar 29026}
with
\mathbf{\mathchar 29013}\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}^{\mathchar 29037\mathchar 8706\mathchar 29038}
,
\mathchar 29037\mathchar 12829\mathchar 29038
.
Define a Gaussian matrix
\mathbf{\mathchar 29006}\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}^{\mathchar 29037\mathchar 8706\mathchar 29043}
. Sketch-and-precondition computes a sketch
\mathbf{\mathchar 29006}^{\mathchar 574}\mathbf{\mathchar 29013}
using a random test matrix
\mathbf{\mathchar 29006}
with sketch size
\mathchar 29043\mathchar 12349\mathcal{\mathchar 29007}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29038\delimiter 68408078\mathchar 28943^{\mathchar 28722}\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12828\mathchar 29037
where
\mathchar 28943\mathchar 12850\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28721\delimiter 84054785
. Next, it performs a QR-decomposition
\mathbf{\mathchar 29006}^{\mathchar 574}\mathbf{\mathchar 29013}\mathchar 12349\mathbf{\mathchar 29009}_{\mathchar 29040}\mathbf{\mathchar 29010}_{\mathchar 29040}
of the resulting
\mathchar 29043\mathchar 8706\mathchar 29038
matrix and then uses
\mathbf{\mathchar 29010}_{\mathchar 29040}^{\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28721}
as a preconditioner for
\mathbf{\mathchar 29013}
. This approach significantly reduces the computational cost of QR-decomposition from
\mathcal{\mathchar 29007}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29037\mathchar 29038^{\mathchar 28722}\right\delimiter 84054785
to
\mathcal{\mathchar 29007}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29043\mathchar 29038^{\mathchar 28722}\right\delimiter 84054785
, regardless of the number of rows. This reduction in computational cost is particularly advantageous for highly-overdetermined linear systems, demonstrating the significance of this approach. For more information, we encourage a reader to see

[
19
,
44
]
. This paper introduces the first instance of utilizing the sketch-and-precondition technique on a linear inequality system within the literature.
To explicitly state the algorithmic implementation of storage-friendly PrSKM, the first two steps of Algorithm
1
must be replaced by the following steps:

1.

Generate a Gaussian test matrix
\mathbf{\mathchar 29006}\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}^{\mathchar 29037\mathchar 8706\mathchar 29043}
where
\mathchar 29043
follows
\mathcal{\mathchar 29007}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29038\delimiter 68408078\mathchar 28943^{\mathchar 28722}\right\delimiter 84054785
with
\mathchar 28943\mathchar 12349{{\mathchar 28721\over\mathchar 28722}}
, sketch the matrix
\mathbf{\mathchar 28995}\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}^{\mathchar 29037\mathchar 8706\mathchar 29038}
from
\mathbf{\mathchar 28995}\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}\mathchar 12823\mathbf{\mathchar 29026}
as
\mathbf{\mathchar 29011}\mathchar 12349\mathbf{\mathchar 29006}^{\mathchar 574}\mathbf{\mathchar 28995}
.

2.

\left\delimiter 67482370\mathbf{\mathchar 29009}_{\mathchar 29043}\mathchar 24891\mathbf{\mathchar 29010}_{\mathchar 29043}\right\delimiter 84267779\mathchar 12349\operatorname{\mathchar 29009\mathchar 29010}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathbf{\mathchar 29011}\right\delimiter 84054785

\mathchar 8494

\operatorname{\mathchar 29009\mathchar 29010}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 8705\delimiter 84054785
computes the QR-decomposition of the sketched matrix
\mathbf{\mathchar 29011}
, where
\mathbf{\mathchar 29009}_{\mathchar 29043}\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}^{\mathchar 29043\mathchar 8706\mathchar 29038}

II-F

Block SKM

In two previous sections, we have proposed PrSKM and storage-friendly PrSKM algorithms in order to solve the one-bit polyhedron (
10
) in an asymptotic sample size scenario. It is worth noting that both methods are

row-based
approaches, where at each iteration, the row index of the matrix
\mathbf{\mathchar 29008}_{\mathbf{\mathchar 29049}}
is chosen independently at random. However, the matrix
\mathbf{\mathchar 29008}_{\mathbf{\mathchar 29049}}
in (
10
) has a block structure as formulated in (
11
).
This fact motivates us to investigate the block-based RKA methods to find the desired signal in the one-bit polyhedron
\mathcal{\mathchar 29008}_{\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}}
for further efficiency enhancement. Our proposed algorithm for block systems,
Block SKM
, is motivated by (i) random selection of one block at each iteration, (ii) choosing a subset of rows using the idea of Motzkin sampling, and (iii) updating the solution using the randomized block Kaczmarz method, which takes advantage of the efficient matrix-vector multiplication, thus giving the method a significant reduction in computational cost. Algorithm
2
shows the implementation of our proposed Block SKM method to solve the linear feasibility
\mathbf{\mathchar 28994}\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}\mathchar 12823\mathbf{\mathchar 29026}
with
\mathbf{\mathchar 28994}\mathchar 12349\left\delimiter 67482370\begin{array}[]{c|c|c}\mathbf{\mathchar 28994}^{\mathchar 574}_{\mathchar 28721}&\mathinner{\mathpunct{\mathchar 513}\mathpunct{\mathchar 513}\mathpunct{\mathchar 513}}&\mathbf{\mathchar 28994}^{\mathchar 574}_{\mathchar 29037}\end{array}\right\delimiter 84267779^{\mathchar 574}
and
\mathbf{\mathchar 29026}\mathchar 12349\left\delimiter 67482370\begin{array}[]{c|c|c}\mathbf{\mathchar 29026}^{\mathchar 574}_{\mathchar 28721}&\mathinner{\mathpunct{\mathchar 513}\mathpunct{\mathchar 513}\mathpunct{\mathchar 513}}&\mathbf{\mathchar 29026}^{\mathchar 574}_{\mathchar 29037}\end{array}\right\delimiter 84267779^{\mathchar 574}
where
\mathbf{\mathchar 28994}_{\mathchar 29034}\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}^{\mathchar 29038\mathchar 8706\mathchar 29028}
and
\mathbf{\mathchar 29026}_{\mathchar 29034}\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}^{\mathchar 29038}
for all
\mathchar 29034\mathchar 12850\delimiter 67482370\mathchar 29037\delimiter 84267779
. Note that in step
\mathchar 28724
of Algorithm
2
, the reason behind choosing
\mathchar 29035^{\mathchar 560}\mathchar 12604\mathchar 29028
is due to the computation of
\left\delimiter 67273472\mathbf{\mathchar 28994}_{\mathchar 29034}^{\mathchar 560}\mathbf{\mathchar 28994}_{\mathchar 29034}^{\mathchar 560\mathchar 574}\right\delimiter 84054785^{\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28721}
in the next step (step
\mathchar 28725
). For
\mathchar 29035^{\mathchar 560}\mathchar 12606\mathchar 29028
, the matrix
\mathbf{\mathchar 28994}_{\mathchar 29034}^{\mathchar 560}\mathbf{\mathchar 28994}_{\mathchar 29034}^{\mathchar 560\mathchar 574}
is rank-deficient and its inverse is not available. The Block SKM algorithm can be considered to be a special case of the more general
sketch-and-project
method with a sparse block sketch matrix as defined in
[
39
]
. The convergence of Block SKM algorithm
with the sparse Gaussian sketch in the case of

, respectively. To examine the performance of the Block SKM, we will compare it with the PrSKM, SKM and RKA.

II-G

III-A

Robustness of ORKA Against Noise

Given a linear system of equations
\mathbf{\mathchar 29013}\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}\mathchar 12349\mathbf{\mathchar 29026}
that is highly over-determined and subject to a noise vector
\mathbf{\mathchar 29038}\mathchar 12349\delimiter 67482370\mathchar 29038_{\mathchar 29034}\delimiter 84267779
resulting in a corrupted system of equations
\mathbf{\mathchar 29013}\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}\mathchar 12825\mathbf{\mathchar 29026}\mathchar 8235\mathbf{\mathchar 29038}
.
The convergence rate of the noisy RKA was comprehensively discussed in
[
45
, Theorem 2.1]
for the case of
\mathbf{\mathchar 29013}\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}\mathchar 12825\mathbf{\mathchar 29026}\mathchar 8235\mathbf{\mathchar 29038}
.
The primary contrast between the convergence rates of RKA
and noisy RKA, as demonstrated in
[
45
, Theorem 2.1]
, lies in the second term of convergence rate
\mathchar 28948^{\mathchar 28722}\max_{\mathchar 29034}{{\mathchar 29038^{\mathchar 28722}_{\mathchar 29034}\over\left\delimiter 0\mathbf{\mathchar 29045}_{\mathchar 29034}\right\delimiter 0^{\mathchar 28722}_{\mathchar 28722}}}
. This term indicates the degree to which the error in the corrupted system
\mathbf{\mathchar 29013}\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}\mathchar 12825\mathbf{\mathchar 29026}\mathchar 8235\mathbf{\mathchar 29038}
deviates from the main solution.

Drawing inspiration from the convergence rate of the noisy RKA,
we can similarly derive the convergence rate of noisy RKA in the case of noisy linear system of inequalities

\mathbf{\mathchar 28995}\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}\mathchar 8235\mathbf{\mathchar 29038}\mathchar 12823\mathbf{\mathchar 29026}
using the following proposition:

.

\mathchar 29034

\mathbf{\mathchar 29038}

\mathbb{\mathchar 28997}\left\{\left\delimiter 0\bar{\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}}_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 8704\widehat{\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}}\right\delimiter 0_{\mathchar 28722}\right\}\mathchar 12820\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28721\mathchar 8704{{\mathchar 28721\over\mathchar 28948^{\mathchar 28722}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathbf{\mathchar 28995}\right\delimiter 84054785}}\right\delimiter 84054785^{{{\mathchar 29033\over\mathchar 28722}}}\penalty\ \left\delimiter 0\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}_{\mathchar 28720}\mathchar 8704\widehat{\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}}\right\delimiter 0_{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28948\max_{\mathchar 29034}\mathchar 28941_{\mathchar 29034}\mathchar 24891

Based on the noisy one-bit polyhedron (

III-B

Upper Quantile-Based ORKA

In Section
III-A
, the robustness of the noisy RKA in the presence of a small perturbation has been discussed. However, as can be observed in the convergence rate of the noisy RKA (
23
), a large perturbation in the linear feasibility
\mathbf{\mathchar 28995}\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}\mathchar 12823\mathbf{\mathchar 29026}
can lead to a significant error in the input signal recovery. In such a scenario, an accurate input signal recovery is quite a challenging task. In this section, our goal is to propose a novel RKA-based algorithm which is robust to impulsive noise.
The noisy linear inequality feasibility problem is defined as

\mathbf{\mathchar 28995}\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}\mathchar 8235\mathbf{\mathchar 29038}\mathchar 12823\mathbf{\mathchar 29026}
,
where
\mathbf{\mathchar 29038}
is the noise of our system.
If the noise does not have a significant impact on the inequality, we can rephrase the system with noise as
\mathbf{\mathchar 28995}\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}\mathchar 12823\mathbf{\mathchar 29026}
and solve it using the RKA. However, if the noise has the potential to alter the direction of the inequalities, we need to handle it differently. In this section,
specifically, we introduce an algorithm that identifies the “orthants” that are immune to corruption
(where noise cannot change their direction), and only incorporate them in the updates of RKA.

The probability of orthants that are corrupted with noise is formulated as the following upper quantile

\mathchar 28939_{\mathchar 29034}\mathchar 12349\operatorname{\mathchar 29008\mathchar 29042}\left\delimiter 67273472\delimiter 69632778\mathbf{\mathchar 29027}_{\mathchar 29034}\mathchar 24891\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}\delimiter 86414091\mathchar 8704\mathchar 29026_{\mathchar 29034}\mathchar 12821\mathchar 8704\mathchar 29038_{\mathchar 29034}\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 24891

(24)

where

\mathchar 29026_{\mathchar 29034}
and
\mathchar 29038_{\mathchar 29034}
are
\mathchar 29034
-th
elements of
\mathbf{\mathchar 29026}
and
\mathbf{\mathchar 29038}
, respectively. By using this formulation, we may be able to determine a threshold for our residuals
\left\{\delimiter 69632778\mathbf{\mathchar 29027}_{\mathchar 29034}\mathchar 24891\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}\delimiter 86414091\mathchar 8704\mathbf{\mathchar 29026}_{\mathchar 29034}\right\}
that can be used to identify the corrupted ones. The threshold is calculated based on the empirical
\mathchar 29041
-quantile of the noise:

\mathcal{\mathchar 29009}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}\right\delimiter 84054785\triangleq\mathchar 29041\text{-quantile}\left\{\left\delimiter 69640972\delimiter 69632778\mathbf{\mathchar 29027}_{\mathchar 29034}\mathchar 24891\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}\delimiter 86414091\mathchar 8704\mathchar 29026_{\mathchar 29034}\right\delimiter 69640972\mathchar 24891\penalty\ \mathchar 29034\mathchar 12850\delimiter 67482370\mathchar 29037\delimiter 84267779\right\}\mathchar 314

(25)

If a residual exceeds this threshold, it suggests that the noise may not have been strong enough to change its direction. By applying the thresholding process, we ensure that a sufficient distance is maintained between
\delimiter 69632778\mathbf{\mathchar 29027}_{\mathchar 29034}\mathchar 24891\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}\delimiter 86414091
and
\mathbf{\mathchar 29026}_{\mathchar 29034}
to prevent any noise from impacting the inequality system. This helps to preserve the integrity of the solution throughout the algorithm. Assume
\mathbf{\mathchar 29040}_{\mathchar 29034}
is the
\mathchar 29034
-th row of
\mathbf{\mathchar 29008}_{\mathbf{\mathchar 29049}_{\mathchar 29050}}
randomly chosen at each iteration
\mathchar 29033
, the proposed algorithm for the noisy one-bit sampled systems (
22
),
upper quantile-based ORKA
is written as follows: (i) update the RKA projection if
\left\delimiter 69640972\delimiter 69632778\mathbf{\mathchar 29040}_{\mathchar 29034}\mathchar 24891\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 86414091\mathchar 8704\mathchar 29042^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 352\delimiter 84054785}_{\mathchar 29034}\mathchar 28956^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 352\delimiter 84054785}_{\mathchar 29034}\right\delimiter 69640972\mathchar 12821\mathcal{\mathchar 29009}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}_{\mathchar 29033}\right\delimiter 84054785
, and (ii) otherwise, set
\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 12349\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}
.

(a)

Shrinkage of the one-bit polyhedron (
10
) in blue, ultimately placed within the unit ball of the nuclear norm

shown with black cylindrical region and its red contours, when the number of constraints (samples) grows large. The arrows point to the half-space associated with each inequality constraint. The evolution of the feasible regime is depicted with increasing samples in three cases: (a) and (d) small sample-size regime, constraints not forming a finite-value polyhedron; (b) and (e) medium sample-size regime, constraints forming a finite-volume polyhedron, parts of which are outside the cylinder; (c) and (f) large sample-size regime, constraints forming a finite-volume polyhedron inside the nuclear norm cylinder, making its constraint redundant. IV
Accelerated Algorithms for One-Bit Sensing Problem

We analyze the impact of coarse quantization on two vital inverse problems: the low-rank matrix sensing problem and the compressed sensing problem, considering two scenarios: (i) sample abundance and (ii) sample restriction.

IV-A

Problem Formulation for Low-Rank Matrix Recovery

The problem of the low-rank matrix sensing is formulated as:

\displaystyle\text{find}\quad\mathbf{\mathchar 29016}\mathchar 12850\mathchar 28682_{\mathchar 29027}\quad\text{subject to}\quad\mathcal{\mathchar 28993}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathbf{\mathchar 29016}\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathbf{\mathchar 29049}\mathchar 24891\penalty\ \operatorname{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 29025\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29035}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathbf{\mathchar 29016}\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12820\mathchar 29042\mathchar 24891

(26)

where
\mathbf{\mathchar 29016}\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}^{\mathchar 29038_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 8706\mathchar 29038_{\mathchar 28722}}
is the matrix of unknowns,
\mathbf{\mathchar 29049}\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}^{\mathchar 29038}
is the measurement vector, and
\mathcal{\mathchar 28993}
is a linear transformation such that
\mathcal{\mathchar 28993}\mathchar 12346\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}^{\mathchar 29038_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 8706\mathchar 29038_{\mathchar 28722}}\mathrel{\mathchar 567\mathchar 545}\mathbb{\mathchar 29010^{\mathchar 29038}}
.
In general,
\mathchar 28682_{\mathchar 29027}
can be chosen such as the set of semi-definite matrices, symmetric matrices, upper or lower triangle matrices, Hessenberg matrices and a specific constraint on the matrix elements
\left\delimiter 0\mathbf{\mathchar 29016}\right\delimiter 0_{\mathchar 561}\mathchar 12820\mathchar 28939
or on its eigenvalues, i.e.,
\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 12820\mathchar 28943
where
\left\{\mathchar 28949_{\mathchar 29033}\right\}
are eigenvalues of
\mathbf{\mathchar 29016}
. The nuclear norm minimization alternative of the problem is given by
[
47
,
4
]
:

\displaystyle\underset{\mathbf{\mathchar 29016}\mathchar 12850\mathchar 28682_{\mathchar 29027}}{\textrm{minimize}}\quad\left\delimiter 0\mathbf{\mathchar 29016}\right\delimiter 0_{\mathchar 8511}\quad\text{subject to}\quad\mathcal{\mathchar 28993}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathbf{\mathchar 29016}\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathbf{\mathchar 29049}\mathchar 314

(27)

In this problem, the feasible set
\mathcal{\mathchar 28998}_{\mathbf{\mathchar 29016}}
is obtained as

\mathcal{\mathchar 28998}_{\mathbf{\mathchar 29016}}\mathchar 12349\left\{\mathcal{\mathchar 29008}^{\mathchar 8511}_{\mathbf{\mathchar 29016}}\mathchar 8796\mathchar 28682_{\mathchar 29027}\right\}
,
where
\mathcal{\mathchar 29008}^{\mathchar 8511}_{\mathbf{\mathchar 29016}}
is defined as follows

\mathcal{\mathchar 29008}^{\mathchar 8511}_{\mathbf{\mathchar 29016}}\mathchar 12349\left\{\mathbf{\mathchar 29016}\mathchar 12906\left\delimiter 0\mathbf{\mathchar 29016}\right\delimiter 0_{\mathchar 8511}\mathchar 12820\mathchar 28956\right\}\mathchar 24891\quad\mathchar 28956\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}^{\mathchar 8235}\mathchar 314

(28)

Next, we will apply ORKA to (
27
) to make its costly constraints redundant by using abundant number of one-bit samples
\mathchar 29037^{\mathchar 560}
.

In low-rank matrix sensing, the linear operator
\mathcal{\mathchar 28993}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathbf{\mathchar 29016}\right\delimiter 84054785
is obtained as

\mathcal{\mathchar 28993}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathbf{\mathchar 29016}\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349{{\mathchar 28721\over\sqrt{\mathchar 29038}}}\left\delimiter 67482370\operatorname{\mathchar 29012\mathchar 29042}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathbf{\mathchar 28993}^{\mathchar 574}_{\mathchar 28721}\mathbf{\mathchar 29016}\right\delimiter 84054785\mathinner{\mathpunct{\mathchar 513}\mathpunct{\mathchar 513}\mathpunct{\mathchar 513}}\operatorname{\mathchar 29012\mathchar 29042}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathbf{\mathchar 28993}^{\mathchar 574}_{\mathchar 29038}\mathbf{\mathchar 29016}\right\delimiter 84054785\right\delimiter 84267779^{\mathchar 574}\mathchar 24891

(29)

where
\mathbf{\mathchar 28993}_{\mathchar 29034}\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}^{\mathchar 29038_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 8706\mathchar 29038_{\mathchar 28722}}
is the
\mathchar 29034
-th sensing matrix. The one-bit polyhedron for the low-rank matrix sensing is given by

\mathcal{\mathchar 29008}^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29005\delimiter 84054785}\mathchar 12349\left\{\mathbf{\mathchar 29016}^{\mathchar 560}\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}^{\mathchar 29038_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 8706\mathchar 29038_{\mathchar 28722}}\mathchar 12906\mathchar 29042^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 352\delimiter 84054785}_{\mathchar 29034}\operatorname{\mathchar 29012\mathchar 29042}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathbf{\mathchar 28993}^{\mathchar 574}_{\mathchar 29034}\mathbf{\mathchar 29016}^{\mathchar 560}\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12821\mathchar 29042^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 352\delimiter 84054785}_{\mathchar 29034}\mathchar 28956^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 352\delimiter 84054785}_{\mathchar 29034}\right\}\mathchar 12826\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}^{\mathchar 29038_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 8706\mathchar 29038_{\mathchar 28722}}\mathchar 24891

(30)

for all
\mathchar 29034\mathchar 12850\delimiter 67482370\mathchar 29038\delimiter 84267779\mathchar 24891\mathchar 352\mathchar 12850\delimiter 67482370\mathchar 29037\delimiter 84267779
. Writing the update process of ORKA in matrix form yields the following representation:

\mathbf{\mathchar 29016}_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 12349\mathbf{\mathchar 29016}_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 8235{{\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29042^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 352\delimiter 84054785}_{\mathchar 29034}\mathchar 28956^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 352\delimiter 84054785}_{\mathchar 29034}\mathchar 8704\mathchar 29042^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 352\delimiter 84054785}_{\mathchar 29034}\operatorname{\mathchar 29012\mathchar 29042}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathbf{\mathchar 28993}^{\mathchar 574}_{\mathchar 29034}\mathbf{\mathchar 29016}_{\mathchar 29033}\right\delimiter 84054785\right\delimiter 84054785^{\mathchar 8235}\over\left\delimiter 0\mathbf{\mathchar 28993}_{\mathchar 29034}\right\delimiter 0^{\mathchar 28722}_{\mathrm{\mathchar 28998}}}}\mathbf{\mathchar 28993}_{\mathchar 29034}\mathchar 314

(31)

The convergence rate of this process is equivalent to that of RKA. Denote the update process in (
31
) by
\mathbf{\mathchar 29016}_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 12349\mathrm{\mathchar 29003\mathchar 28993}_{\mathchar 29042}\delimiter 67273472\mathbf{\mathchar 29016}_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 84054785
.
One can similarly utilize other variants of RKA, PrSKM and Block SKM, and generalize the update process (
31
) as
\mathbf{\mathchar 29016}_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 12349\mathrm{\mathchar 29003\mathchar 28993}_{\mathchar 29040}\delimiter 67273472\mathbf{\mathchar 29016}_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 84054785
and
\mathbf{\mathchar 29016}_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 12349\mathrm{\mathchar 29003\mathchar 28993}_{\mathchar 29026}\delimiter 67273472\mathbf{\mathchar 29016}_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 84054785
.

A numerical investigation of (
30
)
reveals that by increasing the number of samples, the space formed by the intersection of linear constraints can fully shrink to the desired signal
\mathbf{\mathchar 29016}
inside the feasible region of (
28
) which is shown by the cylindrical space
[
4
]
—see Fig.
2
for an illustrative example of this phenomenon. As can be seen in this figure, the blue lines displaying the linear feasibility form a finite-volume space around the original signal displayed by the yellow circle inside the cylinder (the elliptical region) by growing the number of threshold sequences or one-bit samples. In (a)/(d), constraints are not enough to create a finite-volume space, whereas in (b)/(e) such constraints can create the desired finite-volume polyhedron space which, however, is not fully inside the cylinder. Lastly, in (c)/(f), the created finite-volume space shrinks to be fully inside the cylinder. The theoretical discussion regarding the required number of one-bit samples
\mathchar 29037^{\mathchar 560}
to accurately recover the low-rank matrix from one-bit samples will be presented in Theorem
4
.

IV-B

ORKA With Low-Rank Matrix Factorization

Although Kaczmarz algorithms use a simple and low-complexity update process, the computational cost can still be prohibitively large for a matrix
\mathbf{\mathchar 29016}\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}^{\mathchar 29038_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 8706\mathchar 29038_{\mathchar 28722}}
when
\mathchar 29038_{\mathchar 28721}
and
\mathchar 29038_{\mathchar 28722}
are large values, typically requiring
\mathcal{\mathchar 29007}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29038_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 29038_{\mathchar 28722}\right\delimiter 84054785
operations. To address this issue, we employ the well-known
low-rank matrix factorization
technique. Instead of using the full matrix
\mathbf{\mathchar 29016}
, we use its low-rank factorization
\mathbf{\mathchar 29016}\mathchar 12349\mathbf{\mathchar 29004}\mathbf{\mathchar 29015}^{\mathchar 574}
, where
\mathbf{\mathchar 29004}\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}^{\mathchar 29038_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 8706\mathchar 29042}
and
\mathbf{\mathchar 29015}\mathchar 12850\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}^{\mathchar 29038_{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 8706\mathchar 29042}
are low-rank factors. The advantage of this approach is that the economical representation of the low-rank matrix results in lower storage requirements, and lower per-iteration computational cost
[
48
]
. It also makes the method more scalable to larger problem sizes, especially when using iterative feasibility problem solver like ORKA. In this section, we explore the integration of low-rank matrix factorization with ORKA and the alternating minimization (AltMin) algorithm discussed in
[
49
]
.

The one-bit polyhedron
\mathcal{\mathchar 29008}^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29005\delimiter 84054785}

\mathcal{\mathchar 29008}^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29005\delimiter 84054785}_{\mathchar 28720}\mathchar 12349\left\{\mathbf{\mathchar 29004}\mathchar 24891\mathbf{\mathchar 29015}\mathchar 12906\mathchar 29042^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 352\delimiter 84054785}_{\mathchar 29034}\operatorname{\mathchar 29012\mathchar 29042}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathbf{\mathchar 28993}^{\mathchar 574}_{\mathchar 29034}\mathbf{\mathchar 29004}\mathbf{\mathchar 29015}^{\mathchar 574}\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12821\mathchar 29042^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 352\delimiter 84054785}_{\mathchar 29034}\mathchar 28956^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 352\delimiter 84054785}_{\mathchar 29034}\right\}\mathchar 24891

for all

IV-C

Curse of Dimensionality: SVP-ORKA

While acquiring a large number of samples is not typically an issue for one-bit sampling, it is still practical to avoid using excess samples, particularly in signal processing applications where access to sufficient measurements may be limited. Adhering to the law of parsimony, “
Entia non svnt mvltiplicanda præter necessitatem
,” i.e., entities should not be multiplied beyond necessity, this section focuses on developing ORKA to facilitate low-rank matrix sensing with a reduced number of one-bit samples.

Define
\mathchar 29042

as the predefined rank
of the unknown matrix
\mathbf{\mathchar 29016}
.
In order to obtain the solution within a reduced number of samples in the polyhedron
\mathcal{\mathchar 29008}^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29005\delimiter 84054785}
defined in (
30
), we impose
a rank constraint,
\operatorname{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 29025\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29035}\delimiter 67273472\mathbf{\mathchar 29016}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12820\mathchar 29042
,
to shrink the entire space, as shown by the following polyhedron:

\mathcal{\mathchar 29008}^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29005\delimiter 84054785}_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 12349\left\{\mathbf{\mathchar 29016}^{\mathchar 560}\mathchar 12850\mathcal{\mathchar 29008}^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29005\delimiter 84054785}\mathchar 12906\operatorname{\mathchar 29042\mathchar 29025\mathchar 29038\mathchar 29035}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathbf{\mathchar 29016}^{\mathchar 560}\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12820\mathchar 29042\right\}\mathchar 12826\mathbb{\mathchar 29010}^{\mathchar 29038_{\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 8706\mathchar 29038_{\mathchar 28722}}\mathchar 314

(35)

To tackle this problem, we apply the singular value projection (SVP) to ORKA. The operator
\mathchar 29008_{\mathchar 29042}

calculates the
\mathchar 29042
largest singular values of a matrix and subsequently rewrites its SVD based on these
\mathchar 29042
singular values and their corresponding singular vectors
[
48
]
. Similar to Section
IV-A
, denote
\mathrm{\mathchar 29003\mathchar 28993}_{\mathchar 29042}\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 8705\delimiter 84054785
as the update process of ORKA using the RKA defined in (
31
). Through the integration of SVP into each iteration of ORKA, we can achieve the following update process:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\mathbf{\mathchar 29018}_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 12349\mathrm{\mathchar 29003\mathchar 28993}_{\mathchar 29042}\delimiter 67273472\mathbf{\mathchar 29016}_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 24891\\
\mathbf{\mathchar 29016}_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 12349\mathchar 29008_{\mathchar 29042}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathbf{\mathchar 29018}_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 314\end{array}\right\mathchar 314

(36)

The first step of the update process (

),
\mathbf{\mathchar 29018}_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 12349\mathrm{\mathchar 29003\mathchar 28993}_{\mathchar 29042}\delimiter 67273472\mathbf{\mathchar 29016}_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 84054785

\mathbf{\mathchar 29018}_{\mathchar 29033\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 12349\mathrm{\mathchar 29003\mathchar 28993}_{\mathchar 29040}\delimiter 67273472\mathbf{\mathchar 29016}_{\mathchar 29033}\delimiter 84054785

IV-F

Judicious Sampling With Adaptive Thresholding for ORKA

By the spirit of using the iterative RKA, suitable time-varying sampling thresholds can be selected in order to enhance the recovery performance. To capture the desired signal
\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}
more efficiently, the right-hand side of the inequalities in (
10
), i.e.
\operatorname{\delimiter 69640972\mathchar 29029\mathchar 29027}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathbf{\mathchar 29010}_{\mathbf{\mathchar 29049}}\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 8716\operatorname{\delimiter 69640972\mathchar 29029\mathchar 29027}\left\delimiter 67273472\bm{\mathchar 28672}\right\delimiter 84054785
, must be determined in a way that each associated hyperplane passes through the desired feasible region within
\mathcal{\mathchar 28998}_{\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}}
. Accordingly, we propose an iterative algorithm generating adaptive sampling thresholds to accurately obtain a good solution. To have a smaller area of the finite-volume space around the signal
\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}
, one can somehow choose thresholds to reduce the distances between
the desired point and the associated hyperplanes in (
10
). To do so, we update the time-varying thresholds
as

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\bm{\uptau}^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 352\delimiter 84054785}_{\mathchar 29035\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28721}\mathchar 12349\mathbf{\mathchar 28993}\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}_{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 8704{{\mathchar 28721\over\mathchar 28722}}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathbf{\mathchar 29042}^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 352\delimiter 84054785}\mathchar 8716\bm{\mathchar 28943}^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 352\delimiter 84054785}_{\mathchar 29035}\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 24891\\
\bm{\mathchar 28943}^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 352\delimiter 84054785}_{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 12349\mathbf{\mathchar 29042}^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 352\delimiter 84054785}\mathchar 8716\left\delimiter 67273472\mathbf{\mathchar 28993}\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}_{\mathchar 29035}\mathchar 8704\bm{\uptau}_{\mathchar 29035}^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 352\delimiter 84054785}\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 24891\end{array}\right\mathchar 314

(41)

where
\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}_{\mathchar 29035}
and
\bm{\uptau}_{\mathchar 29035}^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 352\delimiter 84054785}
denote the
\mathchar 29035
-th updates of
\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}
and
\bm{\uptau}^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 352\delimiter 84054785}
in our proposed adaptive threshold design algorithm, respectively.

V
Finite Volume Property:

Theoretical Guarantees for One-bit Sensing

It has been demonstrated that in the ditherless scenario, and if the measurements are bounded within a specific set with high probability, there exists a subset of hyperplanes such that the radius of the cell containing
\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}
induced by these hyperplanes is bounded
[
50
]
. In this section, we demonstrate an alternative approach to determining an upper bound. Instead of confining measurements to a bounded space, we leverage the one-bit polyhedron derived from the dithering scheme. Our method involves approaching the average of distances between the signal and surrounding hyperplanes toward its mean. We establish that as the number of samples increases, the finite volume or cell created around the signal diminishes.
We will introduce the concept of FVP and subsequently obtain the required number of one-bit samples
\mathchar 29037^{\mathchar 560}
to capture a solution inside a ball centered at
\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}
.
We begin this section by providing the following definitions:

Definition 1
.

Define the distance between a signal
\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}\mathchar 12850\mathcal{\mathchar 29012}
and the hyperplanes of the polyhedron (
10
) as

\mathchar 29028^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 352\delimiter 84054785}_{\mathchar 29034}\mathchar 12349\left\delimiter 69640972\delimiter 69632778\mathbf{\mathchar 29025}_{\mathchar 29034}\mathchar 24891\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}\delimiter 86414091\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28956^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 352\delimiter 84054785}_{\mathchar 29034}\right\delimiter 69640972\mathchar 24891\penalty\ \mathchar 29034\mathchar 12850\delimiter 67482370\mathchar 29038\delimiter 84267779\mathchar 24891\penalty\ \mathchar 352\mathchar 12850\delimiter 67482370\mathchar 29037\delimiter 84267779\mathchar 314

(42)

Then, we denote the average of such distances by

\mathchar 29012_{\mathrm{\mathchar 29025\delimiter 69640972\mathchar 29029}}\delimiter 67273472\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349{{\mathchar 28721\over\mathchar 29037\mathchar 29038}}\mathchar 4944\displaylimits_{\mathchar 352\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 29037}\mathchar 4944\displaylimits_{\mathchar 29034\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28721}^{\mathchar 29038}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29028^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 352\delimiter 84054785}_{\mathchar 29034}\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 314

(43)

It is essential to clarify that in our analysis, we adopt the worst-case scenario for the distance
between the desired point and the solutions. This approach considers the solution to lie precisely on the hyperplanes. However, it is crucial to note that in reality, the solution to a linear inequality
system can exist anywhere between the desired point and the hyperplane. Utilizing the average of distances, we formulate the FVP and its theorems for both arbitrary and structured sets. As the number of samples increases, and the average of distances approaches its mean, we obtain sufficient samples to construct a finite volume and locate the solution within a ball. This concept is expounded upon in our theorems.

Definition 2

(Consistent Reconstruction Property)
.

Define a signal as
\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}\mathchar 12850\mathcal{\mathchar 29012}
. Denote
\bar{\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}}\mathchar 12850\mathcal{\mathchar 29012}
as a solution obtained by an arbitrary reconstruction algorithm addressing the one-bit feasibility problem. Then, such a reconstruction algorithm is said to be consistent when

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathchar 29043\mathchar 29031\mathchar 29038}\left\delimiter 67273472\delimiter 69632778\mathbf{\mathchar 29025}_{\mathchar 29034}\mathchar 24891\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}\delimiter 86414091\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28956_{\mathchar 29034}^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 352\delimiter 84054785}\right\delimiter 84054785

\displaystyle\mathchar 12349\operatorname{\mathchar 29043\mathchar 29031\mathchar 29038}\left\delimiter 67273472\delimiter 69632778\mathbf{\mathchar 29025}_{\mathchar 29034}\mathchar 24891\bar{\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}}\delimiter 86414091\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28956_{\mathchar 29034}^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 352\delimiter 84054785}\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 24891

(44)

for all
\mathchar 29034\mathchar 12850\delimiter 67482370\mathchar 29038\delimiter 84267779\mathchar 24891\mathchar 352\mathchar 12850\delimiter 67482370\mathchar 29037\delimiter 84267779
.

Note that the sign preservation (consistency) mentioned in our work, as stated in Definition
2
, is equivalent to the solution provided by a linear feasibility solver, denoted as
\bar{\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}}
, satisfying all the given inequalities, meaning
\bar{\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}}\mathchar 12850\mathcal{\mathchar 29008}_{\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}}
. In Section
V-A
, we aim to demonstrate that having a sufficient number of samples guarantees the formation of a finite volume of intersection of hyperplanes. This, in turn, implies that the solution will be confined within a ball of radius
\mathchar 28954
. We will establish the relationship between this radius and FVP distortion in Section
V-B
.

Definition 3

(Isotropic Property)
.

A random vector
\mathbf{\mathchar 29025}
is said to be isotropic if its covariance matrix is the identity matrix; i.e.

\mathbb{\mathchar 28997}\left\{\mathbf{\mathchar 29025}\mathbf{\mathchar 29025}^{\mathrm{\mathchar 29000}}\right\}\mathchar 12349\mathbf{\mathchar 29001}\mathchar 314

(45)

A random matrix
\mathbf{\mathchar 28993}
is said to be isotropic if all its rows are isotropic.

In our proofs, we establish that the sole assumption on the sampling matrix is to be isotropic. With this assumption, we derive a concentration inequality for the average of distances
\mathchar 29012_{\mathrm{\mathchar 29025\delimiter 69640972\mathchar 29029}}
. This inequality serves as the basis for providing an upper bound on the recovery error
\mathchar 28954
.

V-A

Finite Volume Property

In the following theorem, we derive a concentration inequality for the average of distances in a probability distribution. This inequality reveals that the upper bound of the average of distances is contingent on the signal norm, establishing an embedding which we call it the FVP. Initially demonstrated for arbitrary sets, we subsequently extend this theorem to encompass various well-known structured sets:

V-B

Recovery Performance

In the following proposition, we illustrate that the upper bound of the recovery error in one-bit sensing is contingent on the FVP distortion parameter
\mathchar 28943
:

Proposition 2
.

Under assumptions of Theorem
3
, the following upper recovery bound holds for all
\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}\mathchar 24891\bar{\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}}\mathchar 12850\mathcal{\mathchar 29003}
satisfying the consistent reconstruction property in Definition
2
:

\delimiter 69645069\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}\mathchar 8704\bar{\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}}\delimiter 69645069_{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 12820\mathchar 28954\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28724\sqrt{\mathchar 28943\mathchar 28949}\mathchar 24891

(59)

with a probability exceeding
\mathchar 28721\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28722\mathchar 29029^{\mathchar 8704{{\mathchar 29027\mathchar 28943^{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 29037^{\mathchar 560}\over\mathchar 28722\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29003\mathchar 8235{{\mathchar 28949\over\sqrt{\log\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28722\delimiter 84054785}}}\right\delimiter 84054785^{\mathchar 28722}}}}
.

The proof of Proposition
2
is presented in Appendix
G
.

Corollary 1
.

The rate of reduction for the FVP distortion
\mathchar 28943
concerning
\mathchar 29037^{\mathchar 560}
is characterized at most by
\mathcal{\mathchar 29007}\left\delimiter 67273472\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29037^{\mathchar 560}\right\delimiter 84054785^{\mathchar 8704{{\mathchar 28721\over\mathchar 28723}}}\right\delimiter 84054785
.

Proof:

According to Theorem
3
and Proposition
2
, for an arbitrary set, the required number of one-bit samples has to meet the following bound:

\mathchar 29037^{\mathchar 560}\gtrsim\mathchar 28943^{\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28723}\mathchar 28941^{\mathchar 28722}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathcal{\mathchar 29003}\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 24891

(60)

which completes the proof.
∎According to the aforementioned proposition,
reducing the FVP distortion or approaching the mean of the average of distances results in a diminished finite volume around the signal.
In simpler terms, this proposition underscores the correlation between the average of distances and the finite volume created by hyperplanes around the signal.

When a solution fails to meet the consistent reconstruction property or cannot satisfy certain inequalities in the linear feasibility problem, the Hamming distance emerges in the upper bound of the recovery error, as outlined in the following proposition:

Proposition 3
.

Under assumptions of the FVP, if a solution
\bar{\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}}
does not meet the consistent reconstruction property in Definition
2
, the following upper recovery bound holds for all
\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}\mathchar 24891\bar{\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}}\mathchar 12850\mathcal{\mathchar 29003}
with a probability exceeding
\mathchar 28721\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28722\mathchar 29029^{\mathchar 8704{{\mathchar 29027\mathchar 28943^{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 29037^{\mathchar 560}\over\mathchar 28722\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29003\mathchar 8235{{\mathchar 28949\over\sqrt{\log\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28722\delimiter 84054785}}}\right\delimiter 84054785^{\mathchar 28722}}}}
:

\delimiter 69645069\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}\mathchar 8704\bar{\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}}\delimiter 69645069_{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 12820\mathchar 28954\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28724\sqrt{\mathchar 28943\mathchar 28949}\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28722\sqrt{\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28721\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28949^{\mathchar 28722}\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 29028_{\mathrm{\mathchar 29000}}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathbf{\mathchar 29042}\mathchar 24891\bar{\mathbf{\mathchar 29042}}\right\delimiter 84054785}\mathchar 24891

VI
Convergence Analysis of Proposed Algorithms

In this section, leveraging FVP theorems, we delve into the convergence rate of the proposed randomized algorithms.

VI-A

Recovery Error Upper Bound for ORKA

As the scaled condition number is the central parameter governing the recovery error of the RKA or its variants, in the following theorem we will evaluate the scaled condition number of the matrix
\mathbf{\mathchar 29008}_{\mathbf{\mathchar 29049}}
defined in (
11
) to unveil the connection between the convergence bounds of the RKA (or its variants) and ORKA. Throughout the remainder of this section, we simplify notation by representing
\mathbf{\mathchar 29008}_{\mathbf{\mathchar 29049}}

\mathbf{\mathchar 29008}

Theorem 8
.

\mathbf{\mathchar 28993}\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}\mathchar 12349\mathbf{\mathchar 29049}

is equal to that of
\mathbf{\mathchar 28993}
,

\mathchar 28948\left\delimiter 67273472\mathbf{\mathchar 29008}\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12349\mathchar 28948\left\delimiter 67273472\mathbf{\mathchar 28993}\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 314

(64)

The proof of Theorem

\mathbf{\mathchar 28993}\mathchar 12349\mathbf{\mathchar 29001}

\tilde{\bm{\mathchar 28682}}\mathchar 12349\left\delimiter 67482370\begin{array}[]{c|c|c}\bm{\mathchar 28682}^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28721\delimiter 84054785}&\mathinner{\mathpunct{\mathchar 513}\mathpunct{\mathchar 513}\mathpunct{\mathchar 513}}&\bm{\mathchar 28682}^{\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 29037\delimiter 84054785}\end{array}\right\delimiter 84267779^{\mathchar 574}\mathchar 24891\quad\tilde{\bm{\mathchar 28682}}\mathchar 12850\{\mathchar 8704\mathchar 28721\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28720\mathchar 24891\mathchar 28721\}^{\mathchar 29037\mathchar 29038\mathchar 8706\mathchar 29038}

Based on Theorem

Define

\displaystyle\mathbb{\mathchar 28997}\left\{\left\delimiter 0\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 8704\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}\right\delimiter 0_{\mathchar 28722}\right\}\mathchar 12820\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28721\mathchar 8704{{\mathchar 28721\over\mathchar 28948^{\mathchar 28722}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathbf{\mathchar 28993}\right\delimiter 84054785}}\right\delimiter 84054785^{{{\mathchar 29033\over\mathchar 28722}}}\left\delimiter 0\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}_{\mathchar 28720}\mathchar 8704\widehat{\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}}\right\delimiter 0_{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 8235\mathchar 28954\mathchar 24891

Proof:

As demonstrated in

]
, the solution obtained from RKA lies in the space formed by the hyperplanes of the linear inequality problem with the following convergence rate:

\displaystyle\mathbb{\mathchar 28997}\left\{\left\delimiter 0\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 8704\widehat{\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}}\right\delimiter 0_{\mathchar 28722}\right\}\mathchar 12820\left\delimiter 67273472\mathchar 28721\mathchar 8704{{\mathchar 28721\over\mathchar 28948^{\mathchar 28722}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathbf{\mathchar 28993}\right\delimiter 84054785}}\right\delimiter 84054785^{{{\mathchar 29033\over\mathchar 28722}}}\left\delimiter 0\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}_{\mathchar 28720}\mathchar 8704\widehat{\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}}\right\delimiter 0_{\mathchar 28722}\mathchar 24891

is a point inside the space created by a polyhedron

\operatorname{\delimiter 69640972\mathchar 29039\mathchar 29036}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathcal{\mathchar 29008}_{\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}}\right\delimiter 84054785\mathchar 12818\operatorname{\delimiter 69640972\mathchar 29039\mathchar 29036}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathcal{\mathchar 28994}_{\mathchar 28954}\delimiter 67273472\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}\delimiter 84054785\right\delimiter 84054785
, we then have
\widehat{\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}}
lying within the ball around the desired point
\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}
, i.e.,
\widehat{\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}}\mathchar 12850\mathcal{\mathchar 28994}_{\mathchar 28954}\left\delimiter 67273472\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}\right\delimiter 84054785

and

\displaystyle\mathbb{\mathchar 28997}\left\{\left\delimiter 0\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 8704\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}\right\delimiter 0_{\mathchar 28722}\right\}

\displaystyle\mathchar 12349\mathbb{\mathchar 28997}\left\{\left\delimiter 0\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}_{\mathchar 29033}\mathchar 8704\widehat{\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}}\mathchar 8235\widehat{\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}}\mathchar 8704\mathbf{\mathchar 29048}\right\delimiter 0_{\mathchar 28722}\right\}
